CHAPTER IX FRACTIONAL EQUATIONS 

WE have just learned how to perform ordinary operations with fractions. These we are now to apply in solving equations and problems which contain fractions. A large number of problems require a knowledge of fractional equations before they can be solved. 

89. Fractional Equations Having Monomial Denominators. 

In section 51 we learned the use of axioms in solving equations. One of these, the axiom of multiplication, we use to solve an equation containing fractions. 

If both members of an equation are multiplied by the same quantity, the equation remains true.

x 5 x 

Solve + = x 1. 3 9 Solution: Here we multiply both members of the equation by 9, the L. C. D. (lowest common denominator), as follows:

9+ 3 5 x obtaining 3x5x9x9. 9(x 1), 

x = 

9.

By transposing and combining terms. x = 9, root. By dividing both members by 1. 

The purpose in multiplying both members of the equation by 9 is to remove the fractions from the equation. To do 184

FRACTIONAL EQUATIONS 

185 this we have to multiply by some number into which each of the given denominators can be divided evenly, so that the denominators will divide out. The best number to choose for this purpose is the lowest common multiple of the denominators.

We solve equations containing fractions by the following Rule. (1) Remove fractions from the equation by multiplying both members by the lowest common denominator of all the fractions. (2) Solve the resulting simple equation by the rule stated in section 54.

In applying the multiplication axiom to the solution of fractional equations, we should learn to perform our divisions mentally. 

Illustrative example : 2 16 

Solve the equation - + x 15 19 3 x The lowest common multiple of the denominators is 15 x. 

Without writing 15 x beside each fraction, we may mentally divide 15 x by x, obtaining 15. This result must be multiplied by the numerator 2. In like manner we divide 15 x by 15, obtaining x, which we multiply by 16. Then, dividing 15 x by 3 x, we obtain 5, which is multiplied by 19. The resulting equation is 

30+ 16 x = 95. Hence 

16 x = 65. x = 416. 

This process of mental division is described by the 

Rule. Divide the lowest common multiple of the denominators by the given denominator, and multiply the result by the numerator.

95. Formulas as Fractional Equations. As we said in section 63, the formula is the most important type of literal equation. Formulas containing fractions are common in algebra, geometry, and physics. They sometimes make trouble, because pupils think of them as something complicated. But they are solved just as if the unfamiliar π's, V's, C's, S's, l's, and r's were the common a's, b's, x's, and y's.

Miscellaneous Problems 

1. A man willed half his farm to his wife, one fourth to his son, one fifth to his daughter, and the balance, which was 

15 acres, to his cousin. How many acres in the farm? 

2. What sum of money increased by of itself and by $25 equals $200.50? 

3. A 15-foot length is to be divided into two parts such that one part shall be of the length of the other part. What eab shall be the length of each part? 

4. A man spent of his money and 6 dollars more, and found that he had 8 dollars more than half of it left. How many dollars had he at first? 

5. A man lost of his fortune and $500, and found that he had of his original fortune left. How much money had he at first? 

6. John is now half as old as his father; twelve years ago he was one third as old. How old is each? 

7. Harry is as old as his sister. Twenty years hence he will be as old. How old is each? 

8. A sum of $1500 was to be divided equally among a certain number of persons. If there had been 5 persons more, each would have received only as much. How many persons were there?

CHAPTER X GRAPHS OF SIMPLE LINEAR EQUATIONS. SYSTEMS 

WE have studied simple equations with one unknown. 

We are ready to proceed to our study of equations having two unknowns. However, before we go on with this study we must learn to picture simple equations in graphs, just as in section 9 we made graphs of formulas. As fractions. occur so often in these equations, we do not draw their graphs until we have finished fractional equations. 

96. Graphs of Simple Equations. The main thing in all algebraic graphs is the principle of dependence, which we studied in Chapters I and II. The condition that makes treatment by the algebraic graph possible is that there shall be two changing quantities (variables), one of which is a function of (depends upon) the other. This we have already seen in the formula graph, section 9. 

Let us study these graphs through 

Illustrative examples: 

I. Let us draw the graph of the equation y = 2 x. Here you see the value of y depends on the value of x, hence we have the condition which makes an algebraic graph possible. 

As both x and y may have negative as well as positive values, we should draw the axes so that the origin is near the center of the graph sheet. The horizontal axis should be lettered XX' and the vertical axis YY', as indicated. Their intersection should be marked 0.

In locating points for the graph, values of x are laid off on the horizontal or X-axis, and values of y on the vertical 205

ELEMENTARY ALGEBRA 

or Y-axis. Positive values of x negative values to the left of O. -X- 6 51 --3- -2- -1 -1- 

EX 

-3- are laid off to the right and Positive values of y are laid off above and negative values below 0. 

Since y 2x always, the points must be so located that the y value. of each one is twice its x value. For example, 1 and y at P₁, x = 1, 2; at P2, x 2 and y = 4; at P3, x = at P 49 =- 2. 

3 and y = 6; x = 1 and 

Notice that the graph of y = 2x is a straight line.

We shall now graph a more difficult equation. 

II. Graph the equation 2x + 3y = 6. When we solve for y, we obtain 6 - 2 x 3 Now, if x is given a succession of values, say 0, 1, 2, 3, 4, by substituting these values in the equation y 6 2 x 

3 we obtain a corresponding set of values for y, namely, 2, 3, 3,0,-, etc. That is, the value of y depends on the value of x. 

We may arrange these values in tabular form as follows: 1 2 3 4 

1 2 - 3 2 14444 sako 

105 4 Values of x are laid off on the horizontal axis, called the x-axis, positive values to the right and negative to the left.

GRAPHS OF SIMPLE LINEAR EQUATIONS 

207 Ad

In like manner values of y are laid off on the vertical or y-axis, positive values above the origin and negative values below. Each pair of values of x and y enables us to locate one point on the graph.

(3.4 abscissa 2,abscissa3 ordinate +5 +2 -ordinate (2/3) bscissa (3,0) ordinate +5Y' 

GRAPH OF EQUATION: 2x + 3y = 6 

To locate the point where x = 0, y = 2, written (0, 2) we locate a point whose distance to the right is 0, and whose distance up is 2.

The point (1, 4) may be located by measuring off one unit to the right and 1½ units up.

The point (2, 3) is located by measuring off a distance of 

2 units to the right and of a unit up.

In like manner we continue with the other points until Ad

ELEMENTARY ALGEBRA 

we come to the point (- 3, 4), which may be located by measuring off 3 units to the left, and 4 units up. 

The distances which we have measured off and which are indicated by the dotted lines in the drawing are called the coördinates of the points. The horizontal, or x-coördinate, is called the abscissa, while the vertical, or y-coördinate, is called the ordinate.

If, now, we join these points, we find that our graph is a straight line. It is a general principle that every simple equation in two unknowns represents a straight line. For this reason it is called a linear equation.

Heretofore we have referred to equations of the first power or degree of x and y as simple equations. However, they are not always simple, but they can always be represented by a straight line. For this reason it is more accurate to refer to them as linear equations.

Since we now know that we shall obtain a straight line for every such case, we do not need to locate as many points as in the above example. In fact only two points are necessary to determine a straight line and one other to check it. If the abscissa and ordinate, or coördinates, of any point are given, we can locate that point on the chart field. For example, a point whose abscissa is 2 and whose ordinate is 

4 is represented as (2, — 4). 

Locating such a point is usually called plotting that point. 

Plotting two points enables us to graph a linear equation so that we can find any corresponding values of x and y.

All the problems which we have solved before this chapter have contained only one unknown quantity. But there are many problems which we can solve much more simply by the use of equations containing two or more unknowns. 

We shall now take up equations containing two unknowns, and learn how to solve problems by the use of such equations. 

97. The Indeterminate Equation. Let us consider the equation 3x+2y= 4, an equation of the first degree in two unknown quantities, x and y.

ELEMENTARY ALGEBRA 

The equation is said to be of the first degree because each term contains only one unknown, and each unknown is of the first power only. = - A pair of values which will satisfy the equation is called a set of roots. Thus, x = 2, , y 

1 is a set of roots for the equation 3x+2y= 4, for, by substituting these values, we obtain 6 2 4, which is an identity. Test and determine whether the following sets of values are roots of this equation: 

4; x = 0, y 

12; x = 4, y = — 2. x = 1, y

You may determine many more sets of values which will satisfy the equation by simply substituting any convenient number for one of the unknowns, say y, and solving the equation for x. Thus, if 5 is substituted for y, the equation becomes 3x + 10 = 4, and, solving, we obtain x = 2. 

Hence, x 2 and y = 5 is another set of values that will satisfy the equation. =- By substituting for y the values indicated and obtaining the corresponding values for x, we make a table of values which will satisfy the equation. يع 1 4 0 21 1 8 4 4 2 50 10 8 11 The number of such values is indefinite, that is to say, as many may be obtained as are desired. Because it is impossible to determine from the equation which particular pair of values is meant, since there is an indefinite number of pairs which satisfy the equation, such an equation is said to be indeterminate.

A comparison of these tables shows that the set of values x = 2, y = 3 satisfies both equations. If it were feasible for us to complete these tables, writing all possible pairs of values, we should find no other set of values which would satisfy both equations. Such a pair of equations is called a system of equations, and the set of values which satisfies both equations constitutes a set of roots for the system. The system is then said to be solved.

In solving systems of equations like the above it is not necessary to go through the long process just explained in order to obtain the pair of values which will satisfy both equations. We may obtain the roots much more easily by several methods, one of which is the graphic method.

99. Graphic Solution of Linear Systems. Let it be required to solve by graphic methods the following 

Illustrative example: 

2x-3y=1 5x+2y = 12. 

Solution: Plotting at least three points for each equation, we construct the graph of these equations on the same axes. Y'

The coördinates of every point on the first graph are the values of x and y which satisfy the first equation. The coordinates of every point on the second graph are the values of x and y which satisfy the second equation. If the lines intersect, the point of intersection is on both lines; hence, its coördinates satisfy both equations and constitute the solution of the system.

GRAPHS OF SIMPLE LINEAR EQUATIONS 213 

Check by substituting the results obtained in both the given equations.

101. Elimination by Substitution. This method appears to have been first used by Sir Isaac Newton.

HISTORICAL NOTE 

Sir Isaac Newton was not only one of the greatest mathematicians the world has ever produced, but he was also one of its greatest scientists and philosophers. 

He was born in Lincolnshire, England, in 1642. His father died shortly before Newton was born, but left the widow comfortably situated, so that Isaac had an opportunity to acquire an education. As a child he was very frail, however, and gave little promise of any unusual intellectual attainments. 

When he was 18 years old, he entered Cambridge University, and it was here that the real genius of the man began to manifest itself. He mastered Euclid (equivalent to what we now call plane geometry) in a single reading, and in fact regarded the whole subject as self-evident. After that he read all the mathematical literature available with the greatest relish, and soon began to add brilliant discoveries of his own. 

Among the greatest discoveries of this wonderful man, we may mention the binomial theorem, by means of which any binomial may be raised to any required power, and the laws of motion and of universal gravitation. The influence of these discoveries on the sciences, especially physics and astronomy,

was very great. Newton appears to have been the first to use the substitution method of elimination. 

Newton was a man of the greatest modesty, so that he hesitated to publish his discoveries, and it was only by the insistence of his friends and pupils that they were given to the world. Perhaps his greatest achievement was a method now called calculus. 

As a result of his reluctance to tell of his discoveries this was not known to the outside world for a long time. 

Newton was for many years Professor of Mathematics at Cambridge University, and afterwards held important government positions. He died in London in 1727 and is buried in 

Westminster Abbey.

Newton's greatest discoveries are contained in the "Principia" published in 1687, which, in the words of the great scientist and mathematician Laplace, has "a lasting preeminence over all other productions of the human mind."

Now that we have studied equations through systems and fractional linear equations, there is just one more step to prepare us for the climax of our study, the solution of quadratic equations. This step is the mastery of powers and roots. 

Powers have already been treated (section 31); the roots we are now to discuss are not the roots of equations, but square and cube roots and so forth.

By studying these illustrations we may infer the following 

Rule. To raise an algebraic quantity to a given power (1) Raise the numerical coefficient to the required power. (2) Multiply each of the exponents of the given quantity by the exponent of the power to which it is to be raised. 

The law of exponents in finding powers may be expressed in symbols thus, (xm)n = xmn 227

106. Finding Roots of a Quantity. The square root of a quantity is one of the two equal factors of that quantity. 

The cube root of a quantity is one of the three equal factors of the quantity. A similar definition holds for higher roots. 

Since (3x)2 9 x2 and (- 3 x)2 also equals 9 x², it follows from the above definition that both 3 x and 3 x are square 

9 roots of 9 x². We may therefore state the following 

Principle. - Every algebraic number or expression has two square roots, numerically equal, but opposite in sign. 

Since (2x)3 = 8 x3 and (-2x)3 = 8 x3 we see that the cube root of 8 x³ is 2 x and the cube root of 8 x³ is 2 x. 

A quantity, therefore, has but one real cube root, which has the same sign as the quantity.

107. Radical Signs. The extraction of a root is often indicated by the sign V , called the radical sign. When written as above it indicates square root. Thus, √16 x¹y is read "the square root of 16 x¹y6" and means that we are to find the square root of that quantity. Similarly, V8 x6 the cube root of 8 x6 and 81 12 means "the means 66 

fourth root of 81 x12." The small figure at the upper left-hand corner of the radical sign is called the index of the root, and shows what root is to be found. In the square root sign the index 2 is implied.

HISTORICAL NOTE 

The earliest instance of the use of the radical sign to indicate a root to be extracted occurs in an arithmetical work by the Frenchman Nicholas Chuquet. This work was known as Le 

Triparty and was written in 1484. Chuquet also used indices in the modern sense to indicate the root to be taken. 

108. Law of Signs in Finding Roots. The principle gov erning signs in the extraction of square root, which was explained in section 106, holds for any root whose index is an even number, and the principle explained for cube root holds for any root whose index is an odd number. 

We can now state the following Rule. To extract the root of a monomial, (1) find by trial the root of the numerical coefficient. (2) Divide the exponent of each literal factor by the index of the root. (3) Annex these literal factors with their new exponents to the root of the numerical coefficient. (4) Prefix the sign if the index is even. Prefix the given sign if the index is odd. 

The sign ± is read "plus or minus." 

In general Vam = an

ELEMENTARY ALGEBRA Illustrative examples: 

I. The square root of 4 x4y10 is ± 2 x²y³.

II. The cube root of 

27 x y⁹ is 

3 x²y³. 

III. The fourth root of 16 x8y20 is 2 x2y5.

109. Square Root of a Trinomial. — A binomial expression is never a perfect square, unless it contains radicals, and its square root can, therefore, not be found exactly. 

A trinomial square is, however, a very common algebraic expression. Such a square is formed by squaring a binomial, as explained in section 73. By the rule there stated (3 x 4 y)² = 9 x²- 24 xy + 16 y². The square root of 

9x224 xy + 16 y² is, therefore, 3 x 4 y. 

(3x-4 y) is also a square root of 9 x² - 24 xy + 16 y², so that we ought to inclose 3 x 4 y in a parenthesis preceded by the sign; thus, ±(3 x − 4 y). 

The square root of a trinomial square like the above may always be found mentally by the following 

Rule. (1) Take the square root of the first term of the trinomial and the square root of the last term. (2) Make the first of these terms positive, and give the second the sign of the middle term of the trinomial. (3) Inclose the resulting binomial in a parenthesis preceded by the sign.

In applying the above rule the trinomial must first be arranged in descending powers of some common letter.

State the square roots of each of the following trinomials: 

1. x² + 6x + 9.

112. Approximate Measurement. The figures in the preceding table are accurate to the nearest thousandth. 

Could you measure the length of your desk correctly to a thousandth of an inch? To a hundredth of an inch? If you were very careful, you might measure it correctly to a tenth of an inch. Boys and girls with good eyes and good rulers should be able to do that. Suppose you found your desk to be 26.7 inches long. Do you think its real length would be exactly that? Could you be sure that it was not really 26.72 or possibly 26.69?

When we say that the length of the desk is 26.7 inches we mean that that is its length to the nearest tenth. If it were possible for us to measure it correctly to hundredths, it might

be found to be anywhere between 26.65 and 26.74 inclusive.

Similarly if you measured the width of the desk you would, perhaps, obtain 14.3 inches, and this might mean anything between 14.25 and 14.34 inclusive.

Now suppose you were asked to find the area of this desk in square inches. The exact product of 26.7 and 14.3 is 

381.81, so you see that if the length were exactly 26.7 inches and the width were exactly 14.3 inches, the area would be exactly 381.81 square inches. However, since 26.7 and 14.3 are not exact do you think it reasonable to claim that the area is exactly 381.81 sq. in., or that it is reasonable to attempt to express the area to hundredths of a square inch? Isn't it more sensible to express this result to the nearest tenth and call the answer 381.8 square inches? For some purposes it would be sufficient to express the answer to the nearest square inch, calling the result 382 square inches. Few of you could do better than that in an actual measurement problem. Exercises 1. Measure the length and width of your algebra to the nearest tenth of an inch. Find its area to the nearest tenth of a square inch.

113. Rounding Off Numbers. It is often unnecessary to give exact values, especially when dealing with large numbers. 

Most business people use what are called round numbers or approximate values. For example if the exact amount is $5,975,876, it is usually called $6,000,000 approximately. In population problems such approximations are often used: the population of a city may be called 1,000,000 when it really is a little below or a little above 1,000,000.

Numbers are usually rounded off to the nearest hundred, thousand, or million. However, exact figures are often

necessary, as in business tabulations. Again in baseball we may need to carry the work out to four or possibly five decimal places in order to determine the leading team or leading batter of the league. For example, one man might have a batting average of .39435 and another .39436. Which would lead? You will have to use your own common sense whether you are to work with absolute exactness or with round numbers when you get out in the world of business.

Geometry Problems 

115. Use of Square Root in Solving Right Triangles.- 

The adjoining triangle ABC is called a right triangle because it has a right angle at C. This angle at C = 90°. In all such triangles the square of the side opposite the angle of 90° (called the hypotenuse) is always equal to the sum of the squares of the other two sides (called the legs) or, stated as an equation, c2 a² + b², or, extracting the square root of both members, c = √ a² + b². 

HISTORICAL NOTE 

90°

This important mathematical principle may be stated thus: 

In a right triangle the sum of the squares of the legs is equal to the square of the hypotenuse.

The discovery of this principle was made by Pythagoras, a Greek mathematician, who was born in Samos in 569 B.C. After having studied at Thebes and Memphis, he returned to Samos, where he established a school of mathematics and philosophy. 

Not being very successful here, he went to Tarentum and later to Croten in southern Italy. Here he gathered around him many followers and established a school which became very influential, in both educational and political circles.

The members of the Pythagorean society had all things in common, held their discoveries secret, and lived very simple and austere lives. One member of the society, Hippasus, was drowned by his fellow members for having boasted about his discoveries.

The high character of the Pythagoreans, the nature of their philosophy, and the secret character of their organization enabled them to gain much political influence, and made them many enemies. 

Finally, in 500 B.C., Pythagoras and many of his followers were murdered by a mob.

This principle, called for Pythagoras the " Pythagorean principle," is one of the most used 

PYTHAGORAS 

propositions in all mathematics, especially with practical and applied problems relating to heights and distances. Some of its applications are illustrated in many of the following problems.

CHAPTER XII  

So far our work has been focused on the linear equation, its development through the formula, its solution, and its graphing. When we supplement our knowledge of linear equations with what we learned in the last chapter about powers and roots, we are ready to solve quadratic equations. 

116. Kinds of Quadratic Equations. A quadratic equa tion is one which after simplifying contains the second power (square) of the unknown quantity but no higher power. When a quadratic equation is of the form ax² + bx + c = 0, it is called a complete quadratic equation. This means that it contains three terms, one having the square of the unknown, another having the first power of the unknown, and a third not having the unknown. 

If either the term containing the first degree of the unknown or the constant term (without the unknown) or both are lacking, it is called an incomplete quadratic equation. Thus 2x27x+6=0 is a complete quadratic equation, while 3x² + 5 x = 0, 2 x230, and 5 22 0 are incomplete quadratic equations. 

117. Solving Incomplete Quadratics.

118. Solving Complete Quadratics by Completing the 

Square. The simplest way to solve quadratics is by factoring, but not all quadratics can be factored. We shall now study a method by means of which any quadratic equation can be solved. It is called completing the square. 

Solve x²+6 x = 16. 

Solution: If 9 is added to the left member, the left member then becomes a perfect square. If it is added to the left member, it must be added to the right member also. We get the 9 by taking one half of 6 (the coefficient of x), if the coefficient of x² is 1, and squaring it. We then obtain 

22+6x+9= 25. x + 3 5 x = 2, root .. x = 2 or 2

4+12+9 = 25 25 = 25 x+35. By extracting the root of both members. x + 3 

= - 5 8, root 

Check: x=- 8 64-48+9 25 25 = 25

ELEMENTARY ALGEBRA This illustrates the following 

Rule. (1) Remove parentheses, clear the equation of fractions, and combine similar terms. (2) Reduce the equation to the form x² + px q. (3) Complete the square by adding to both members of the equation the square of one half of the coefficient of x. (4) Extract the square root of both members of the equation, prefixing the sign to the right member. 

(5) Solve the resulting equations. 

(6) Check each root in the original equation.

All complete quadratic equations may be changed to the form ax² + bx + c = 0.

Number Problems Leading to Quadratic Equations 

You should check each result in the problem as stated, and reject any which are meaningless, or which fail to satisfy the conditions of the problem. 

1. The product of two consecutive numbers is 34 more than the next consecutive number. Find them. 

2. The sum of two numbers is 13 and the sum of their squares is 85. What are the numbers? 

3. There are two numbers whose difference is 5, and the difference of their squares is 45. What are they? 

4. One number is two larger than a second number.

The sum of the squares of these numbers is 19 more than the product of the numbers. Find the numbers. 

5. The sum of the reciprocals of two consecutive even numbers is 1. Find the numbers.

Hint: The reciprocal of a number is 1 divided by that number. 

6. A boy set out 600 tomato plants. The number of plants in each row was 10 less than twice the number of rows. 

How many plants in each row? 

7. A man bought two farms for $4800 each. One farm contained 20 acres more than the other, and cost him $8 less per acre. How many acres in each farm? 

8. The number of things possible to happen may be denoted by x(x − 1), or by 272. Find the value of x. 

9. A boy was sent with $2.10 to buy oranges; he found the price 3 cents per dozen higher than he expected, so he bought one half dozen fewer than he intended to buy and received 1 cent in change. What price did he pay for the oranges?

10. The expenses of a camping trip for a troop of Boy

Scouts were 60 dollars. If there had been three more in the party, it would have cost each boy 1 dollar less. How many were in the party?

96 square feet. Find the length and width.

13. If three equal squares are placed side by side in a row, the result is a rectangle whose area in square rods is 15 times its perimeter in rods. Find a side of one of the squares. 

14. The altitude of a certain triangle is twice its base. The area of the triangle is 64. Find the base and the altitude.

Hint: The area of a triangle is the product of the base and altitude. altitude base

20. One base of a trapezoid is three times the altitude. 

The other base is 15. The area is 9. Find the altitude and the other base. 

21. Suppose that we were to make a square uncovered box from a piece of metal. The metal is square, but square corners such as are indicated by the drawing are cut out.

2 inches deep. It is to contain 128 cubic inches. How large shall the metal square be cut from which the box is to be made?

23. If it requires 216 square inches of cardboard to make a cubical box, what is the length of an edge? 

24. The altitude of a triangle is .5 of the length of the base. The area of the triangle is 6.25. Find the altitude and the base. 

25. A man wishes to build a bin which will hold 9 tons of coal. The bin is to be 6 feet deep and 4 feet longer than it is wide. Allowing 40 cubic feet for 1 ton of coal, what must be the dimensions of the bin? 

26. A piece of tin 9 inches by 12 inches is to be made into an open box with a base of 60 square inches by cutting equai squares from the four corners and then bending up the edges.

What is the size, to the nearest tenth of an inch, of the square cut from each corner?

Find its width and thickness.

2 miles an hour, find the rate of the crew in still water. 

34. A man can row down stream 8 miles and back again in 5 hours. If the stream flows 2 miles an hour, what was his rate in still water? 

35. Two steamers run between places 475 miles apart. 

One goes mile an hour faster than the other and takes 21 hours less time to make the trip. What are the rates of the steamers? 

36. Two men start at the same time from the intersection of two roads, one driving north at the rate of 30 miles an hour, and the other east at the rate of 40 miles an hour. 

In how many hours will they be 250 miles apart? 

122. Systems of Equations, One Linear and One Quad ratic.

Till now all our quadratics have had only one un- 

---

ELEMENTARY ALGEBRA 

known. An equation in two unknowns is said to be of the second degree, or quadratic, if it contains the square of either unknown, or the product of the two. Examples are x² + 2 x + y = 10, x² + y² = 18, xy + 3 y = 0. 

In solving systems one of whose equations is quadratic and the other linear, we should eliminate by substitution, solving the linear equation for one unknown in terms of the other and substituting the result in the quadratic equation. 

The method is shown by the following 

Illustrative example: 2x + y x² + xy 7.

Such systems have, in general, two sets of roots. In order to check the roots of such equations, we should substitute the sets of roots in both original equations.
